In Example A, the joint predicted hypothesis states that the hit rate for positive stimuli is larger than 0.50 in Study 1 and Study 2 and Study 3:

This joint hypothesis can be visualized in a multidimensional parameter space, in which the conjunction of multiple order-constrained hypotheses creates a restricted subspace (i.e., a convex polytope; Heck & Davis-Stober, 2019). For Example A, this subspace corresponds to the pink cube in each of the three panels in the left column of Figure 2; the other shaded volumes represent different joint alternative hypotheses, which we discuss in the next sections.

3.1.2 Example B: Equivalence testing

In Example B, the joint predicted hypothesis states that, in each study, the mean difference in test scores between the identity-primed and the non-primed condition falls within its respective equivalence interval:

The joint predicted hypothesis now forms a cuboid rather than a cube as in Example A (right column of Figure 2), because the equivalence intervals of the studies span different proportions of their parameter ranges.

3.2 Joint alternative hypotheses

Evidence for the joint predicted hypothesis is accumulated in the numerator of BF The denominator of the joint Bayes factor reflects how plausible the data are under the joint alternative hypothesis, which states that the study-specific alternative hypothesis is true in each study:

This joint alternative arises automatically when multiplying the study-specific Bayes factors. Although this follows directly from the formula for the product Bayes factor, the meaning and interpretation of different joint alternative hypotheses have, to the best of our knowledge, not previously been compared and discussed in detail.

We consider three cases: all studies test against the null hypothesis, all studies test against the unconstrained hypothesis, and all studies test against the complement hypothesis. (a) θ ( 1) θ ( 2) 1) Δ ( 2

3.2.1 Example A: Choice probabilities

If all three replication studies test H (t) 1 against the null, H (t) 0 : θ (t) = 0.50, then the joint alternative hypothesis states that the hit rate is 50% in all studies: H (joint) 0 : θ = (0.50, 0.50, 0.50).

The joint null hypothesis corresponds to a single point in the multidimensional parameter space (the blue point in each of the three plots in the left column of Figure 2). If all three studies test against the unconstrained alternative, H (t) u : θ (t) ∈ [0, 1], then the joint alternative hypothesis simply includes all possible constellations of θ (t) across studies, that is, in each study, the parameters can take on any value in the range [0, 1]:

The joint unconstrained hypothesis thus corresponds to the entire joint parameter space (yellow volume in Figure 2a). Finally, if all three studies test against the complement alternative H (t) c : θ (t) ≤ 0.50, then the joint alternative hypothesis states that θ (t) ≤ 0.50 in all studies: or equivalently, θ ∈ [0, 0.50] 3 (green volume in Figure 2b).

3.2.2 Example B: Equivalence testing

In Example B, the joint null states that ∆ (t) = 0 in each study:

As in Example A, this corresponds to a single point in the joint parameter space (the blue point in each of the three plots in the right column of Figure 2). The joint unconstrained hypothesis again covers the entire parameter space (yellow volume in Figure 2d):

The joint complement is the conjunction of all study-specific complements (green volume in Figure 2e):

4 Interpretation of the joint Bayes factor

Because BF (joint) 1,a pits the joint predicted hypothesis against a joint alternative, its correct interpretation depends on which alternative hypothesis is specified. If each study tests the predicted hypothesis against a point null alternative, the resulting joint Bayes factor BF (joint) 1,0 can be interpreted as the relative evidence that the theory holds in each individual study compared to the effect being absent in each study. If we test against the unconstrained hypothesis, then BF (joint) 1,u pits the joint H 1 against all possibilities of parameter combinations across studies.

The joint Bayes factor can then be interpreted as relative evidence that the theory holds in each individual study compared to not having any theoretical account for the data. Framed differently, BF (joint) 1,u quantifies the degree to which imposing the theoretical constraints improves our predictions of the data as compared to not making any predictions at all.

Finally, if each study tests the predicted hypothesis against its complement, the resulting joint Bayes factor BF

5 Computing the evidence for the complete complement

In order for the joint Bayes factor to quantify the relative evidence for the theory against its complement, the complete joint complement H (joint) c * must be considered as the alternative. In this section, we show how the resulting Bayes factor BF (joint) 1,c * can be computed from the study-specific Bayes factors used in standard BES. 3 The computations of BF (joint) 1,c * are based on the encompassing prior approach for order-constrained hypotheses (Klugkist & Hoijtink, 2007; Klugkist et al., 2005), in which each informative hypothesis H i is a nested model within the unconstrained hypothesis. Once a prior distribution is specified across the full parameter space of the unconstrained model (the so-called encompassing prior), BF i,u can be computed as the ratio of two quantities: the fit f i , which is the proportion of the posterior distribution in agreement with the order constraints defined by H i , and the complexity c i , which is the corresponding proportion of the prior distribution. Since the 3 Strictly speaking, the superscript "(joint)" is redundant here because the distinction between the complete and the incomplete complement arises only in the joint parameter space: At the study level, H (t) c is already the complete complement of H (t) 1 . We keep the superscript for consistency with the other joint hypotheses.

complexity of an equality-constrained hypothesis is zero under a continuous prior, this ratio is undefined for such hypotheses, so that the following computations only apply to predicted hypotheses defined by order and interval constraints. For each study t, this yields

To illustrate the following steps concretely, we return to Example A. Suppose the three replications of Bem's Experiment 1 yielded the study-specific Bayes factors BF (1) 1,u = 1.80, BF 1,u = 1.50, and BF (3) 1,u = 0.70.

The first two studies thus provide weak evidence in favor of precognition for positive stimuli, whereas the third slightly favors the unconstrained alternative.

5.1 Step 1: Compute the Bayes factor for the complement in each study

Assume that each study provides only the Bayes factor BF (t) 1,u . First, we obtain, for each study, the Bayes factor BF (t) c,u of the complement against the unconstrained hypothesis. Sometimes, BF (t) c,u is directly available in software output, where the study-specific complement is considered automatically (e.g., the R packages bain by Gu et al., 2024 , BFpack by Mulder et al., 2021 , and multinomineq by Heck, 2019). To manually compute this Bayes factor, it is necessary to derive or compute the complexity c (t) 1 of the predicted hypothesis H (t) 1 , that is, the prior probability P θ (t) ∈ H (t) 1 of the parameters being in the predicted hypothesis space.

Applying the encompassing Bayes factor in Eq. ( 4) for the complement then gives

Returning to Example A, and assuming a uniform prior on θ (t) (or any other prior that is symmetric around 0.50), the complexity of 1 = 0.50 in all three studies, since half of the prior distribution satisfies the order constraint.

Applying Eq. ( 5) to the Bayes factor of Study 1, BF (1) 1,u = 1.80, gives BF (1) c,u = 1 -0.50 • 1.800.50 = 0.20.

Similarly, we obtain BF c,u = 0.50 and BF (3) c,u = 1.30.

5.2 Step 2: Compute the Bayes factor for each part of the complete complement

Knowing both BF (t) 1,u and BF (t) c,u allows us to construct all possible combinations of study-specific predicted and complement hypotheses across studies.

That is, instead of considering only the two parts of the joint parameter space in which either H 1 or H c holds in all studies, we now also consider all combinations in which H 1 holds in at least one, but not all, studies.

To achieve this, we enumerate each possible combination by defining the index set Ω ⊆ {1, . . . , T } as the set of studies in which the complement H (t) c is assumed to hold. In the remaining studies t / ∈ Ω, the predicted hypothesis H 1 is assumed to hold.

For instance, Ω = {1, 3} denotes the case in which the complement holds in Studies 1 and 3 while the theory-derived predictions are correct in all other studies. Since each study can either be included in or excluded from Ω, the number of possible unique sets Ω is |{Ω : Ω ⊆ {1, . . . , T }}| = 2 T.

Each set Ω defines a specific joint hypothesis H Ω that corresponds to a unique non-overlapping region in the multidimensional parameter space.

We now compute a product Bayes factor that quantifies the relative evidence provided by all studies together in favor of the specific hypothesis H Ω relative to the unconstrained hypothesis:

Note that this formulation includes two special cases already discussed above. In the first case, Ω = {1, . . . , T }, the complement H c is assumed to hold in all studies. Using the convention that the product over an empty set equals 1, we obtain the joint Bayes factor that pits the incomplete joint complement (i.e., the conjunction of study complements) against the joint unconstrained hypothesis:

The second case, Ω = ∅, reflects the assumption that H 1 holds in all studies, which yields the joint Bayes factor that pits the joint predicted hypothesis against the joint unconstrained hypothesis: 1,u.

c is assumed to hold, whereas 1 is assumed to hold in the remaining studies.

Because c (t)1 = 0.50 in all studies, Eq. ( 5) implies that BF Table 1 lists the resulting Bayes factors BF (joint) Ω,u computed via Eq. (6).

5.3 Step 3: Compute the Bayes factor for the complete complement

The complete joint complement is defined by all non-empty sets Ω, meaning that the theoretical predictions are incorrect in at least one study. This is best seen in Figure 2c, where the complement consists of seven green cubes for T = 3 studies. The Bayes factor of the complete complement against the joint unconstrained hypothesis is obtained as a weighted average of the Bayes factors BF (joint) Ω,u across all non-empty sets Ω: 4 BF (joint)

The weights π Ω are the prior probabilities of the individual components H Ω under the assumption that the complete complement holds, that is, that the predictions fail in at least one study. These weights express how plausible each pattern of successful and failed predictions across studies is considered a priori, excluding the pattern in which all predictions succeed. There are two natural choices for the weights. Complexity weighting assigns each component a weight equal to its share of the prior mass of the complete complement, where c Ω is the complexity of the component H Ω and c (t) 1 the complexity of the predicted hypothesis in study t.

This corresponds to restricting the encompassing prior to the complete complement. In contrast, equal weighting 4 This also explains why we avoid the term "PBF". Unlike the other joint Bayes factors, BF (joint) 1,c * is not a product of study-specific Bayes factors.

assigns the same weight to each of the 2 T -1 components of the complete complement, so that all patterns in which the predictions fail in at least one study are considered equally plausible a priori.

Since BF joint) 1,u and BF (joint) c * ,u are both defined relative to H (joint) u , the transitivity of Bayes factors yields BF (joint) 1,c * = BF (joint) 1,u /BF (joint) c * ,u .

Substituting the expressions for the weights into Eq. (7) yields simplified expressions for BF (joint) 1,c * that can be computed directly from the study-specific quantities, without summing over all 2 T -1 components (for detailed derivations, see Appendix A). Under complexity weighting, BF (joint) 1,c * then depends only on BF (t) 1,u and c (t) 1 :

Under equal weighting, BF 1,c * depends only on BF (t) 1,c :

The study-specific Bayes factors used in Eq. ( 11) are exactly the ones that are multiplied in BES when testing against the incomplete complement. The two types of weighting yield identical results whenever c (t)1 = 0.50 in all studies, because all components of the complete complement then have the same prior mass c Ω = 2 -T.

5.4 Choosing prior weights for the complete complement

Equal and complexity weighting differ in how the prior mass of the complete complement is distributed across its components. We use equal weighting throughout, because it accords with the premise of BES that each study provides an equally valid test of the same theory. By Eq. ( 11), each study affects BF (joint) 1,c * only through the Bayes factor BF (t) 1,c for the predicted hypothesis against its complement. Therefore, equally strong evidence against the prediction (i.e., the same value BF (t) 1,c < 1) is equally detrimental to the theory, regardless of which study provides it. In contrast, under complexity weighting, the complete complement expects failed predictions primarily in those studies with predicted hypotheses of low complexity (i.e., hypotheses that cover only a small part of the prior), so that evidence against these predictions counts more strongly. For instance, consider two studies whose predicted hypotheses have complexities c1 = 0.10 and c1 = 0.50, and assume that one of them yields BF 1,c = 1/3, whereas the other provides no evidence (BF 1,c = 1). Under equal weighting, BF (joint) 1,c * = 3/7 ≈ 0.43, regardless of which study provides the evidence. Under complexity weighting, the joint Bayes factor is 0.35 if the evidence comes from the study with c1 = 0.10 but 0.49 if it comes from the study with c1 = 0.50.

The asymmetric impact of studies on BF (joint) 1,c * under complexity weighting is defensible if all studies concern the same parameter on the same scale, because a narrower predicted hypothesis then represents a more precise, and hence riskier, prediction. In BES, however, studies often concern different parameters with different ranges, so that differences in complexity need not reflect differences in how precisely the theory is tested. For instance, in Example B, the equivalence intervals differ only because one grade point corresponds to a different number of percentage points in each study's grading system. With the same prior on the mean difference in all three studies, Study 1 would have the smallest complexity, and so complexity weighting would treat it as the most likely site of falsification and let evidence against its prediction count most strongly. Yet there is no substantive reason why a failed prediction in Study 1 should weigh more heavily against the theory than a failed prediction in Study 3. Equal weighting avoids such asymmetries.

5.5 Joint complement hypothesis for multiple predicted hypotheses

So far, we have assumed that each study tests a single predicted hypothesis. However, in some applications, the individual studies may also test multiple competing predicted hypotheses (e.g., qualitatively different sets of order constraints on the parameters). BES can also be applied in these cases, provided that all studies test the same competing theoretical accounts, so that each joint predicted hypothesis represents a substantive theoretical claim. As in the single-hypothesis case, multiplying the study-specific Bayes factors for one of the predicted hypotheses against its complement does not yield a test against the complete complement. Unlike in that case, however, Steps 1 to 3 need to be adapted, because the parameter space of each study is now partitioned into more than two regions, namely, the predicted hypotheses and the complement of their union.

To compare multiple joint predicted hypotheses simultaneously (e.g., via posterior model probabilities), the complete complement of each joint predicted hypothesis is unsuitable, because it contains the other joint predicted hypotheses. Instead, it is more useful to define a single complete complement as the remainder of the joint parameter space once all joint predicted hypotheses are removed (i.e., the complement of their union). It comprises all combinations of study-specific hypotheses in which no single predicted hypothesis holds across all studies. Section S2 of the Supplementary Material (https://github.com/L-Groot/BES) derives the corresponding Bayes factors and posterior model probabilities.

6 Empirical examples

In this section, we investigate the extent to which the different joint alternative hypotheses lead to different conclusions about the theory of interest by applying BES to two empirical examples. In both cases, we computed BF Scheibehenne et al. (2016) investigated whether descriptive social norms increase pro-environmental behavior.

6.1 Hotel towel reuse

They considered four published papers (some with multiple experiments), each of which tested whether normative messages encourage hotel guests to reuse their towels. Each experiment tested the prediction that guests exposed to a descriptive social-norm message are more likely to reuse their towels than guests in the control condition; that is, the proportion of guests reusing their towels is expected to be higher in the experimental than We recomputed the study-specific Bayes factors with the R code of Scheibehenne et al. (2016), which estimates the fit of H1 from 10,000 posterior samples. Due to this random sampling, some BF1,0 differ by 0.01 from the originally reported values (we fixed the random seed for reproducibility, which the original code does not). Joint Bayes factors were computed from the unrounded study-specific Bayes factors.

in the control condition (H 1 : θ e > θ c ). In the majority of experiments (five out of seven), the Bayes factor BF (t) 1,0 favored the null hypothesis that the two proportions are equal (H 0 : θ e = θ c ), some even with strong evidence.

However, when the data from all seven experiments were pooled and analyzed jointly, the resulting BF 1,0 = 36.89 provided strong evidence against the null, in favor of the social-norm hypothesis.

The pooled analysis assumes a fixed-effect model, which implies that all experiments share the same effect size. However, there is considerable heterogeneity across the included studies. For example, in some experiments, the control message contained an appeal to the environmental benefit of towel reuse (Bohner & Schlüter, 2014; Goldstein et al., 2008), whereas in others it simply instructed guests on where to leave their towels if they wished them to be replaced (Schultz et al., 2008). Furthermore, one study presented the descriptive norm together with an injunctive norm (Schultz et al., 2008). In light of this heterogeneity, one may decide against assuming a fixed effect across all experiments and apply BES to synthesize the evidence.

Table 2 shows the study-specific and joint Bayes factors for each of the four joint alternative hypotheses discussed in Sections 3.2 and 5. Only the comparison to the incomplete joint complement favors the joint predicted hypothesis, with BF (joint) 1,c = 2.85 × 10 3 indicating overwhelming support. Thus, the data are much more likely if the theory-derived predictions hold in each experiment than if they are wrong in each experiment.

However, if the substantive question is whether the overall theory holds across all experiments or is incorrect in at least one experiment, the relevant comparison is against the complete complement. In this case, the evidence slightly favors the complete joint complement over the joint predicted hypothesis, BF (joint) 1,c * = 0.36. That is, the data are somewhat more likely if the predictions fail in at least one experiment than if they hold throughout.

Notably, testing against the joint unconstrained hypothesis yields a virtually identical value, BF (joint) 1,u = 0.36. Volker et al. (2022) investigated the effects of network embeddedness on trust and cooperation. Network embeddedness occurs when a person X can access information from third parties about their previous interactions with person Y. The authors tested the overarching hypothesis that network embeddedness promotes trust and cooperation by synthesizing evidence across eight heterogeneous studies. For example, network embeddedness was compared either with no embeddedness or with dyadic embeddedness, in which X can draw upon previous experiences with Y to guide the decision of how much-or whether-to trust them in a current exchange.

6.2 Network embeddedness effects on trust and cooperation

The studies also used different games (e.g., trust game, prisoner's dilemma, investment game) and measured different outcomes (trustfulness, trustworthiness, or cooperation). 3 presents the study-specific Bayes factors reported by the authors, together with the joint Bayes factors that we computed from them. 5 As in the previous example, testing against the incomplete joint complement (i.e., H (t) 1 is false in all studies) provides overwhelming evidence in favor of the joint predicted hypothesis, BF (joint) 1,c = 5.23 × 10 11 . Likewise, testing against the complete joint complement supports the joint predicted hypothesis, though with substantially weaker evidence, BF (joint) 1,c * = 7.24. Again, we observe that the Bayes factor testing against the complete complement is virtually identical to that obtained from testing against the joint unconstrained hypothesis, BF (joint) 1,u = 7.24.

7 Discussion

We showed that the conclusions drawn from Bayesian evidence synthesis (Klugkist & Volker, 2025; Kuiper et al., 2013) depend on the joint alternative hypothesis against which the theory is evaluated. We considered four joint alternative hypotheses: the null, the unconstrained, the incomplete complement, and the complete complement. Among these, we argued that the incomplete joint complement is of limited relevance for theory testing because it rarely represents a substantively interesting claim.

7.1 The incomplete vs. the complete joint complement

Multiplying study-specific Bayes factors that test theoretically derived order constraints against their complements implicitly pits the theory against the incomplete joint complement, which states that the theoretical predictions are wrong in every study. For theory testing, the complete joint complement is more appropriate, because it states that the predictions are wrong in at least one study, that is, that the theory does not hold across all studies. We showed how the Bayes factor against the complete complement can be computed from the same study-specific Bayes factors that are multiplied in standard BES. Importantly, the choice between the incomplete and the complete joint complement matters in practice: In our empirical examples, they led to substantially different-in one case even opposite-conclusions about the theory. 5 We computed the joint Bayes factors from the three-decimal study-specific Bayes factors reported in Table 4 of Volker et al. (2022), the most precise values available. Because these values are rounded, our BF (joint) 1,u differs by 0.03 from the value reported by the authors.

To compute BF (joint) 1,c * , we used Eq. (7), which uses the BF (t) 1,u . Computing it via Eq. (11), which uses the BF (t) 1,c , would instead yield 7.28, but for exact inputs, both expressions yield the same value. The discrepancy between the two types of complements is also reflected in the geometry of the joint parameter space. For more than one study, the incomplete complement is a strict subset of the complete complement.

As studies are added, the former shrinks exponentially, whereas the latter approaches the entire parameter space.

In Example A (Section 2.2.1), the two complements coincide for a single study, each covering 50% of the parameter space; for two studies, they cover 25% and 75%, and for three studies, 12.5% and 87.5%, respectively.

Put differently, the complete complement has a higher complexity, meaning that, under the encompassing prior, the parameters are a priori more likely to fall within the complete complement than the incomplete complement (Klugkist & Hoijtink, 2007).

7.2 Choosing a competing joint hypothesis

Setting aside the incomplete joint complement, we argue that there is no universally correct choice among the remaining three joint alternatives. Which alternative hypothesis is appropriate depends on the scientific question.

The joint null is appropriate when the question is whether an effect is present in all studies or absent in all studies, for example, in clinical trials, where it provides a clear benchmark for concluding that a treatment has no effect. However, neither the joint null nor the joint predicted hypothesis covers more complex scenarios in which the effect is present in some studies but absent in others. In such cases, BF (joint) 1,0 necessarily contrasts two false hypotheses, so that whichever hypothesis is favored by the Bayes factor, the conclusion is misleading.

Moreover, testing against the joint null is subject to an asymmetry: Since Bayes factors based on commonly used alternative priors accumulate evidence against a false point null much faster than evidence for a true one (Johnson & Rossell, 2010), the few studies in which the effect is present can provide overwhelming support for the predictions that outweighs the comparatively weak evidence for the null from the remaining studies.

Contrasting two false hypotheses cannot occur when testing against the complete complement or the joint unconstrained hypothesis. In both cases, the predicted and the alternative hypotheses are exhaustive together, meaning that every possible combination of parameter values across studies is covered by at least one of them: H (joint) u covers the entire joint parameter space on its own, and H (joint) c * covers exactly the part that H (joint) 1 leaves out. Therefore, if the statistical model itself is correctly specified, then at least one of the two compared hypotheses is true, even when the predictions hold in some studies but fail in others. This protects against favoring a false joint predicted hypothesis merely because the alternative is even further from the truth.

In cognitive modeling, this protection is achieved by including a saturated model as a "catch all" alternative, which leaves the choice probabilities unconstrained and can therefore accommodate any data pattern (Lee, 2016). Since all substantive models are nested within it, the saturated model corresponds to the encompassing model in Bayesian tests of order constraints, that is, to the unconstrained hypothesis (Heck et al., 2017). 6 A substantive model is then selected only if it accounts for the data better than the saturated model, which protects against selecting models that do not fit the data in absolute terms (Heck et al., 2017). The complete complement offers the same protection without overlapping with H (joint) 1 , because it accommodates every pattern that the predictions rule out.

In contrast, neither the joint null nor the incomplete joint complement offers such protection, since neither covers mixed patterns.

The unconstrained hypothesis has often been used as the alternative when testing order constraints (e.g., Heck & Davis-Stober, 2019; Hoijtink, 2012; Zwilling et al., 2019). However, the unconstrained alternative has also been criticized because its predictions overlap with the constrained predicted hypothesis (Chandramouli & Shiffrin, 2019; Shiffrin et al., 2016). Two objections follow on this basis, but we argue that neither disqualifies H (joint) u in the context of theory testing.

First, the comparison of the competing models is said to lack a clear qualitative contrast: Whether a parameter falls above or below a certain boundary cannot be answered for values in the shared region and is better addressed by comparing disjoint regions of the parameter space (Shiffrin et al., 2016). We agree that directional questions require disjoint hypotheses, which are obtained in BES by using the complete complement.

If each study is an independent test of the theory, the theory implies that its predictions hold in all studies, so that a violation in any single study counts against the theory. By stating that the predictions fail in at least one study, the complete complement thus asks whether the theory makes any incorrect prediction, which makes it the natural choice for exposing a theory to falsification attempts. However, the comparison against H (joint) u addresses a different question, namely, whether committing to the theoretical predictions improves on making no predictions at all, which is the rationale underlying the encompassing Bayes factor (Hoijtink, 2012; Klugkist & Hoijtink, 2007). Here, the relevant competitor is not the failure of the theory but theoretical agnosticism, and the contrast between committing to the predictions and remaining agnostic is itself a qualitative one. For this question, the overlap between the predicted and the unconstrained hypothesis is not incidental but constitutive:

The comparison is informative precisely because H 1 is a restriction of H u.

Second, because H 1 is nested in H u , the attainable evidence in favor of the predictions is necessarily bounded. Within a single study, 1 , the inverse complexity of the predicted hypothesis, no matter how much data are collected or how strongly the results agree with the predicted order constraints (Heck et al., 2015; Klugkist & Volker, 2025).

In contrast, BF (t) 1,c is unbounded, so the two Bayes factors can diverge substantially when the data are in line with the order constraints (Shiffrin et al., 2016). For a single study, this bound can indeed be restrictive. In BES, however, the relevant bound is 1/c (joint) 1 , and because the joint complexity c (joint)1 = t c (t) 1 shrinks exponentially with the number of studies (cf. the geometric illustration of the joint parameter space in Section 7.1), the bound grows accordingly.

With c (t)1 ≤ 0.50 in each study, the bound is at least 2 T , so that it exceeds 10 with four studies and 100 with seven. The bound thus quickly ceases to be a practical constraint as studies accumulate. 7 Does the choice between H (joint) c * ,u , the ratio of the two Bayes factors, was close to one.

BF

Although neither condition is strictly necessary, together they are sufficient for this agreement (see Section S1.3 of the Supplementary Material). In sum, the agreement in our examples does not imply that the choice between H (joint) u and H (joint) c * is inconsequential in general, so this choice should rest on the substantive question. If it is unclear which alternative is more appropriate, both can be reported.

7.3 Heterogeneity across studies

Irrespective of which alternative hypothesis is used, the joint Bayes factor can obscure heterogeneity in the evidence across studies. In the towel-reuse example (Scheibehenne et al., 2016), BF

7.4 Conclusion

We have shown that one should be careful when multiplying Bayes factors across studies. When doing so, one may inadvertently test the theory of interest against a competing hypothesis that does not reflect a theoretically relevant position. As in any application of Bayes factors, researchers should carefully specify the hypotheses they compare (Heck et al., 2023; Tendeiro & Kiers, 2019). In Bayesian evidence synthesis, it is sensible to test one's predictions not only against the joint null hypothesis, but also against hypotheses that allow for heterogeneity across studies (i.e., for the predictions to hold in some studies but fail in others), such as the complete complement or the unconstrained hypothesis. Whichever alternative is chosen, we recommend reporting the joint Bayes factor alongside the study-specific Bayes factors from which it was computed, so that readers can judge to what extent the aggregate reflects a consistent pattern of evidence.

complement H (joint) c that arises from combining the study-specific complements. However, in this third case, the joint Bayes factor is difficult to interpret. A tempting misinterpretation is that BF (joint) 1,c quantifies the relative evidence for the set of theoretical predictions versus its complement. This interpretation is incorrect because the conjunction of the study-specific complements does not form the complement of H (joint) 1 in the multidimensional parameter space. The mismatch is immediately visible in Panels b and e of Figure 2: Outside the pink cuboids representing H (joint) 1 , large regions of the parameter space are not covered by the green cuboids representing H (joint) c . The joint alternative that arises from multiplying the study-specific BF (t) 1,c is thus an incomplete complement of the theory. Correctly interpreted, BF (joint) 1,c quantifies the relative evidence that the theoretical predictions hold in every study versus that they are false in every study. Yet the latter claim is rarely of substantive interest, because a theory is already called into question if its predictions fail in a single study. Testing the theory against its complement, and thus exposing it to falsification, therefore requires the complete complement. The complete complement of H (joint) 1 covers all combinations of parameter values except those covered by H (joint) 1 . It therefore includes the incomplete complement as the extreme case in which every study-specific complement is true, but also many other combinations, such as H (t) 1 holding in only one or two studies and H (t) c in the remaining ones. In other words, the complete complement states that the theoretical predictions are false in at least one study. Geometrically, it corresponds to the entire joint parameter space minus the joint predicted hypothesis (pink region), as shown in Panels c and f of Figure 2.

For instance, Ω = {2, 3} corresponds to the bottom-right cube, reflecting the hypothesis H Ω that the parameter θ is above 0.50 (as predicted by precognition) only in Study 1, but below 0.50 in Studies 2 and 3.

* with equal weighting from the study-specific BF (t) 1,u , following Steps 1-3 in Section 5. Data and R scripts for the analyses are available at https://github.com/L-Groot/BES.

? Since the two Bayes factors were virtually identical in our empirical examples and the alternatives cover increasingly similar regions of the parameter space as studies are added, one may expect the Bayes factors to converge. However, their ratio, BF c * ,u , need not approach one as the number of studies grows. Whether the two Bayes factors converge depends on the weighting of the components of the complete complement (i.e., the a priori plausibility of different patterns of correct and incorrect predictions across studies) and on the evidence provided by the studies (for details and derivations, see Section S1 of the Supplementary Material). Under complexity weighting, the two Bayes factors always point in the same direction, with BF (joint) 1,c * at least as extreme as BF (joint) 1,u . Moreover, their ratio approaches one as studies are added, unless the posterior concentrates in the joint predicted hypothesis. In contrast, under equal weighting, which we consider more appropriate for theory testing (Section 5.4), neither property holds in general. Depending on the complexities and the evidence, BF * can differ by several orders of magnitude even when many studies are synthesized, and may even point in opposite directions.The complexities and the evidence also explain why the two Bayes factors agreed in our empirical examples. First, all predicted hypotheses had a complexity of 50% (i.e., c (t)1 = 0.50), so that all components of the joint parameter space had the same prior mass. Equal weighting therefore weighted the components of the complete complement in the same way as the unconstrained hypothesis does. As a result, the complete complement differed from the unconstrained hypothesis only in that it excluded the joint predicted hypothesis. Second, because this excluded region received almost none of the prior and posterior probability (prior: 2 -7 ≈ 0.8% and 2 -16 < 0.01%; posterior: about 0.3% and 0.01%), the two Bayes factors agreed: The complete complement contained more than 99% of both the prior and the posterior mass of the unconstrained hypothesis, so that 7 For less precise predicted hypotheses (c (t) 1 > 0.50), the bound grows more slowly. For example, with c (t)1 = 0.90 in all studies, it exceeds 10 only with 22 studies and 100 only with 44.

the theory, yet the study-specific BF (t) 1,u range from 0.04 (strong evidence against the prediction) to 1.97 (weak evidence in favor, close to the attainable maximum of 2). The contrast is even starker for the complement. Although the study-specific BF (t) 1,c range from 0.02 to 75.34, the joint Bayes factor is BF (joint) 1,c * = 0.36. Likewise, in the network-embeddedness example, the BF (t) 1,c range from 0.05 to 2.60 × 10 4 , whereas BF (joint)1,c * = 7.24 indicates moderate evidence. The joint Bayes factor can thus mask effect heterogeneity that shows up as strong evidence in both directions at the study level. Moreover, the joint Bayes factor need not favor the hypothesis supported by most studies, because it depends not only on the direction but also on the magnitude of the study-specific Bayes factors. This was the case in the towel-reuse example: BF * both favored their respective alternatives, while only two of the seven individual experiments did. However, these issues reflect a general limitation of any single summary measure of evidence. As a remedy, researchers should inspect the distribution of study-specific Bayes factors alongside the joint Bayes factor, for example, in a histogram of the study-specific log BF values (for examples with heterogeneous person-specific Bayes factors, seeStephan et al., 2009). Additionally, researchers may plot the study-specific Bayes factors against study characteristics to reveal systematic patterns that can indicate potential moderators.

In the following, we derive simplified expressions for BF (joint) 1,c * under both prior weightings for the complete complement, which can be computed without enumerating the 2 T -1 components. Each derivation first simplifies Eq. (7) for BF (joint) c * ,u and then obtains BF (joint) 1,c * via the transitivity of Bayes factors. The Bayes factor comparing two hypotheses equals the ratio of their Bayes factors against a common reference hypothesis, here the joint unconstrained hypothesis, whose marginal likelihood cancels:

Study-specific and joint Bayes factors testing whether normative messages encourage hotel guests to reuse their towels(Scheibehenne et al., 2016) Study-specific hypotheses: H1 : θe > θc, H0 : θe = θc, Hu : θe, θc ∈ [0, 1], and Hc : θe ≤ θc. FollowingScheibehenne et al. (2016Scheibehenne et al. ( , p. 1043)), θe and θc have independent uniform priors under Hu; hence, c1 = 0.50 in all experiments.

Table 3 :

Study-specific and joint Bayes factors testing whether network embeddedness promotes trust and cooperation(Volker et al., 2022) Evidence against the complete joint complement. Study-specific Bayes factors are taken from Table2inVolker et al. (2022), which reports them rounded to two decimals; joint Bayes factors were computed from the three-decimal values in their Table4. Because the trustfulness and trustworthiness Bayes factors from the same study are based on the same participants, the independence assumption of BES is questionable for these pairs; we nevertheless follow the authors' specification.

We use "BES" to refer to the evidence synthesis method of multiplying Bayes factors across studies (e.g.,Kevenaar et al., 2021;Klugkist & Volker, 2025), rather than to Bayesian meta-analytic methods in general (e.g.,Mulder & Van Aert, 2026; Scheibehenne et al., 2016).

Unlike null-hypothesis significance testing, where the null is tested against "the alternative" (here, H1), we follow the BES literature in treating the null as one type of alternative to the predicted hypothesis.

An unconstrained hypothesis leaves the parameters of a given model free, whereas a saturated model can reproduce any data exactly. Thus, every saturated model is unconstrained, but not every unconstrained model is saturated: In linear regression, for instance, the hypothesis β ∈ R is unconstrained, yet every value of β still implies a linear relationship, so that data following a nonlinear relationship cannot be reproduced. The two coincide for binomial or multinomial models, as in the towel-reuse example, because then the parameters are the cell probabilities, each of which can match any observed relative frequency.
